0: "Indeed, it's true that I looked away from the door for a brief moment." So Atmey looked away from door.

1: "However... Luke Atmey cannot be so easily discombobulated!" (self praising)

2: "Unfortunately, the thief grabbed a weapon from the side and... rendered me senseless."

4: "His first blow struck true! Bam! ...And that's all she wrote."

5: "I put my back to the wall ... but the thief's blow landed upon my third eye."

We need evidence that contradicts any of these statements. Look at evidence descriptions.

Evidence 6: Shichishito: "A ceremonial sword held in the hand of Ami Fey's statue. Wasn't bent before the crime. DeMasque knocked Atmey out during the crime w/ a blow to the back of the head."

This says DeMasque knocked Atmey out with a blow to the back of the head. Testimony 5 says Atmey put his back to the wall to fight, but the thief's blow landed upon his third eye. Third eye is forehead? That's front of head, not back. Contradiction: evidence says blow to back of head; testimony says blow to third eye (front). Spatial reasoning: "If XXX has their back to YYY then XXX cannot see YYY" maybe not relevant. But we have location of blow.

Thus evidence 6 contradicts testimony 5.

Check rule: "If XXX has their back to YYY then XXX cannot see YYY" not directly. But we can use "If XXX is in the same room as YYY then XXX can potentially see YYY". Not needed.

But we can state contradiction: evidence says blow to back of head, testimony says blow to third eye (front). That's spatial contradiction.

Thus answer: evidence 6, testimony 5.

**Step 3:** N/A (no applicable rule).

**Step 3:**  
No explicit rule from the provided list matches this situation, so we rely on ordinary spatial reasoning: a balloon that is **mounted on the back of a truck** cannot be ridden upward to a rooftop because the supporting truck cannot ascend to that height.

**Step 4:**  
Because the balloon is physically tethered to a ground‑based vehicle, Simeon could not have been “riding the balloon to the rooftop” as he claims. This creates a direct contradiction between the testimony and the evidence.

**Step 1:**

Evidence 11 (Meraktis’s Autopsy Report) states the cause of death was “a single bullet to the right temple.”  
These two descriptions of the bullet’s point of impact are incompatible: a frontal forehead wound cannot simultaneously be a wound to the right temple.

**Step 2:**  
The spatial reasoning rules include statements about how an object is hit from a direction (front or behind) and the resulting motion, but none directly address “forehead vs temple.” However, the rule “If XXX is hit from the front then XXX falls backward” can be applied to the testimony’s claim, while the autopsy report gives a side impact, implying a different directional hit.

**Step 3:**  
- Rule: *If XXX is hit from the front then XXX falls backward.*  
  Applying this to the testimony would predict the victim falling backward. The autopsy report, however, indicates a side (right‑temple) wound, which would not be consistent with a frontal hit and the associated backward fall.

**Step 4:**  
Even without a direct rule, the spatial contradiction is clear: the location of the wound (forehead → front) conflicts with the location reported in the autopsy (right temple → side). A single bullet cannot simultaneously strike both points.

Evidence 18 (Stage Set Up Photos) shows a **tall construction screen** positioned **between the stage and the maintenance area**, clearly blocking direct sight lines between those two locations.

**Step 3:**
